## Page 1


Your daily method is clear: 6 pages of vocabulary per day, with every word explained through its meanings and two example sentences that distinguish its usage.

I recommend using this consistent format for every word:

\- Word — meanings; two sentences:

1. Sentence demonstrating meaning A.
2. Sentence demonstrating meaning B or showing how the word differs in context.

For example:

## Day 1 — Vocabulary: A

1\. Abandon — to give up; to leave behind; to act without restraint.

- The sailors had to abandon the ship during the storm.
- At the party, she danced with wild abandon.

2\. Abandonment — the act of leaving something behind; the act of giving something up completely.

- The abandonment of the old factory left the neighborhood deserted.
- The project failed because of the team's abandonment of its original goals.

3\. Abbreviation — a shortened form of a word or phrase.

- “Dr.” is an abbreviation for “Doctor.”
- The abbreviation “ASAP” means “as soon as possible.”

4\. Abeyance — a temporary suspension or postponement of an activity or process.

- The construction project remained in abeyance until the company secured funding.
- The court held the decision in abeyance while reviewing new evidence.

5\. Abide — to tolerate or endure; to remain faithful to or comply with something.

- I cannot abide people who constantly interrupt others.
- All employees must abide by the company's safety regulations.

6\. Ability — the capacity, skill, or power to do something.

- Her ability to solve complex problems impressed the interviewers.
- Learning a language requires the ability to recognize patterns and use them correctly.

### How we'll distinguish meanings

- Meaning 1: A sentence using the word in its most common context.
- Meaning 2: A sentence using a different meaning, nuance, or grammatical pattern whenever possible.
- Vocabulary accuracy: I'll correct incomplete, misleading, or overlapping definitions rather than simply reproduce them.
- Consistency: We'll preserve your original word order and work through six pages daily.

One important point: your list contains some words with multiple meanings, such as abandon, while others, such as abeyance, have a more specialized meaning. I'll make the examples reflect those differences.

Send me your first six pages, and I'll process the complete list in this format, keeping the explanations concise and the sentences useful for English vocabulary building.

## Page 2 

# Page 2 — Vocabulary (Absurd → Accounting)

1\. Absurd — ridiculous; unreasonable; contrary to common sense.

- It is absurd to expect someone to learn an entire language in one day.
- His explanation was so absurd that nobody believed him.

2\. Absurdity — the quality of being ridiculous or illogical; a contradiction of reason.

- The absurdity of his argument became obvious when he contradicted himself.
- She laughed at the absurdity of spending hours looking for glasses that were on her head.

3\. Abundance — a very large quantity; more than enough of something.

- The garden produces an abundance of fresh vegetables every summer.
- Despite living in abundance, he never forgot the difficulties of his childhood.

4\. Abundant — plentiful; available in large quantities; rich in resources.

- Fresh water is abundant in this region because of its many rivers.
- The country has abundant natural resources, including oil and minerals.

5\. Abuse — improper or harmful use; cruel or insulting treatment.

- The abuse of company resources can lead to serious consequences.
- The manager was fired for verbally abusing his employees.

6\. Academic — related to education or scholarship; theoretical rather than practical.

- She has an excellent academic record and consistently earns high grades.
- His proposal sounds interesting in theory, but it has little academic or practical value in solving the problem.

7\. Academy — an educational institution for specialized training; an organization of scholars or professionals.

- He enrolled in a military academy to prepare for a career in the armed forces.
- The national academy of sciences recognizes outstanding scientific achievements.

8\. Accede — to agree to a request or demand; to accept a position or authority.

- The government acceded to the workers' demands after weeks of negotiations.
- She acceded to the presidency of the organization after her predecessor resigned.

9\. Accelerate — to increase speed; to make a process develop faster.

- The driver accelerated to overtake the truck safely.
- The new training program helped accelerate the development of employees' technical skills.

10\. Acceleration — an increase in speed or the rate at which velocity changes; a speeding-up of progress.

- The car's acceleration was impressive, allowing it to reach high speeds quickly.
- The acceleration of technological development has transformed many industries.

11\. Access — the right or ability to enter, approach, or use something.

- Only authorized employees have access to the company's confidential files.
- The new road provides easier access to the remote village.

12\. Accessible — easy to reach, enter, obtain, understand, or communicate with.

- The building is accessible to wheelchair users through a specially designed entrance.
- Although he is a senior executive, he remains accessible to junior employees who need advice.

13\. Accessory — an additional item that complements something; a secondary or nonessential object.

- She bought a leather accessory to match her new handbag.
- A printer is a useful computer accessory, but it is not essential for every user.

14\. Accident — an unexpected event, often causing damage or injury; an unplanned occurrence.

- The driver was injured in a serious road accident.
- Meeting my future business partner at the airport was a complete accident.

15\. Accidental — happening unexpectedly or unintentionally.

- The accidental deletion of the database caused hours of extra work.
- The fire was accidental rather than the result of deliberate destruction.

16\. Accidentally — unintentionally; by chance rather than on purpose.

- I accidentally sent the email to the wrong person.
- While searching for a book, she accidentally discovered an old photograph of her grandparents.

17\. Accommodate — to provide space or assistance; to adapt or adjust to someone's needs.

- The hotel can accommodate up to 200 guests.
- Our team changed its meeting time to accommodate employees working in different time zones.

18\. Accommodation — a place to stay; lodging or living quarters provided for people.

- The university provides affordable accommodation for international students.
- We booked our accommodation near the city center to avoid long commutes.

19\. Accompaniment — something that accompanies or complements another thing; the act of accompanying someone.

- The singer performed with piano accompaniment.
- The teacher's accompaniment during the school trip ensured that the students remained safe.

20\. Accompany — to go somewhere with someone; to occur or exist together with something.

- My brother will accompany me to the job interview.
- High fever may accompany certain infections.

21\. Accomplish — to succeed in doing something; to complete or achieve a goal.

- She worked hard to accomplish her goal of becoming a software engineer.
- We accomplished all the tasks on our project plan before the deadline.

22\. Accomplishment — an achievement; the successful completion of something.

- Graduating with honors was her greatest academic accomplishment.
- Learning to communicate confidently in a foreign language is a significant accomplishment.

23\. Accord — an agreement or settlement; formal agreement between parties or countries.

- The two countries signed a peace accord after months of negotiations.
- After lengthy discussions, both departments reached an accord on the new budget.

24\. Account — a description or record of events; an explanation or reason for something.

- The witness gave a detailed account of what happened during the accident.
- He could not account for the missing money in the company's financial records.

25\. Accountant — a professional who prepares, maintains, examines, or audits financial records.

- The accountant prepared the company's annual financial statements.
- We hired an accountant to review our tax records and identify errors.

26\. Accounting — the system of recording, classifying, analyzing, and reporting financial information.

- Accounting helps businesses track their income, expenses, assets, and liabilities.
- She studied accounting to learn how organizations manage and report their finances.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- Absurd vs. absurdity: Absurd describes something ridiculous; absurdity is the quality or instance of being ridiculous.
- Abundance vs. abundant: Abundance is a noun; abundant is an adjective.
- Accede vs. accelerate: Accede means agree or assume a position; accelerate means speed up.
- Access vs. accessible: Access means the ability or right to enter or use something; accessible describes something easy to reach or use.
- Accident vs. accidental vs. accidentally: noun, adjective, and adverb, respectively.
- Accompany vs. accompaniment: Accompany is a verb; accompaniment is a noun.
- Accomplish vs. accomplishment: Accomplish means achieve; accomplishment is the achievement itself.
- Account vs. accountant vs. accounting: a record or explanation; the professional who handles financial records; the discipline or system of handling those records.

Study tip: Read both sentences aloud for each word, then cover the definitions and explain the difference between the two examples in your own words.

## Page 3

# Page 3 — Vocabulary (Accrue → Address)

1\. Accrue — to accumulate or increase over time; to arise as a result of something.

- Interest will accrue on your savings account every month.
- She gained valuable experience and other benefits that accrued from working abroad.

2\. Accumulate — to collect or gather gradually; to increase in quantity over time.

- He accumulated a large collection of books over twenty years.
- Dust began to accumulate on the furniture because nobody cleaned the room.

3\. Accumulation — the gradual gathering or increase of something; a collected amount.

- The accumulation of unpaid bills eventually caused serious financial problems.
- The accumulation of snow on the roads made driving dangerous.

4\. Accuracy — the degree to which information, measurements, or results are correct.

- The accuracy of the laboratory results was confirmed by repeating the experiment.
- GPS technology has improved the accuracy of location tracking.

5\. Accurate — correct and precise; producing results close to the true value.

- The thermometer provides an accurate measurement of body temperature.
- She is an accurate typist who rarely makes spelling mistakes.

6\. Accusation — a claim that someone has done something wrong or illegal.

- The employee denied the accusation of stealing company property.
- The politician faced accusations of corruption after the investigation.

7\. Accuse — to claim that someone has done something wrong; to blame someone for misconduct.

- The police accused the suspect of committing the robbery.
- Don't accuse your colleague of making a mistake before checking the evidence.

8\. Achieve — to accomplish something through effort, skill, or determination.

- She studied consistently to achieve her goal of passing the examination.
- The company achieved record sales after improving its marketing strategy.

9\. Achievement — something successfully accomplished; an important accomplishment or award.

- Earning a university degree was a major achievement in his life.
- Winning first place in the competition was her greatest sporting achievement.

10\. Acid — a chemical substance that can donate hydrogen ions; sour-tasting or bitter and sarcastic in figurative use.

- Lemon juice contains citric acid, which gives it a sour taste.
- His acid remarks made everyone uncomfortable during the meeting.

11\. Acknowledge — to admit or recognize something as true; to express recognition or gratitude.

- She acknowledged that her original decision had been wrong.
- I would like to acknowledge your support throughout this difficult project.

12\. Acquaint — to make someone familiar with something or provide them with information.

- The manager acquainted the new employees with the company's safety procedures.
- Please acquaint yourself with the application requirements before submitting your documents.

13\. Acquaintance — a person one knows but may not know well; knowledge or familiarity with something.

- I met an old acquaintance at the train station yesterday.
- His acquaintance with computer networking helped him solve the technical problem.

14\. Acquainted — familiar with a person, subject, or situation.

- I am acquainted with several engineers who work in the telecommunications industry.
- Before the interview, make sure you are acquainted with the company's main products.

15\. Acquire — to gain or obtain something through effort, learning, or purchase.

- She acquired advanced programming skills through years of practice.
- The company plans to acquire a smaller competitor next year.

16\. Acquisition — the act of obtaining something; the purchase of one company by another.

- The acquisition of new technical skills improved his employment prospects.
- The company's acquisition of a competitor expanded its market share.

17\. Across — from one side to the other; on or toward the opposite side.

- We walked across the bridge to reach the other side of the river.
- She placed a blanket across the bed before going to sleep.

18\. Activate — to make something operate or become active; to trigger a process.

- You must activate your account before you can use the online service.
- The sensor will activate the alarm when it detects smoke.

19\. Actively — in an energetic, involved, or deliberate manner rather than passively.

- She actively participates in discussions to improve her communication skills.
- The organization is actively searching for qualified software engineers.

20\. Actual — real or existing in fact; relating to what truly happened rather than what was expected.

- The actual cost of the project was higher than the original estimate.
- We need to examine the actual results instead of relying on assumptions.

21\. Actually — in fact; really; used to correct or clarify information.

- I thought the meeting was on Monday, but it is actually scheduled for Tuesday.
- He looks serious, but he is actually very friendly.

22\. Acute — severe or intense; sharp or highly perceptive.

- The patient experienced acute pain after the accident.
- She has an acute sense of observation and quickly notices small details.

23\. Adapt — to change or adjust to suit new conditions, needs, or purposes.

- Employees must adapt to new technologies to remain competitive.
- We adapted the original design to make it suitable for mobile devices.

24\. Adaptation — a change that makes something suitable for new conditions; the process of adjusting.

- The adaptation of the novel into a film attracted millions of viewers.
- The animal's thick fur is an adaptation that helps it survive in cold climates.

25\. Additional — extra; added to what already exists or is available.

- The company requires additional documents before approving the application.
- We hired additional staff to manage the increased workload.

26\. Additive — a substance added to another material to change or improve its properties; something that supplements another thing.

- Food manufacturers sometimes use an additive to preserve freshness.
- The chemical additive improved the durability of the plastic.

27\. Address — to speak formally to an audience; to deal with a problem or direct attention toward something.

- The director will address the employees at tomorrow's meeting.
- We need to address the security weaknesses before launching the application.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- Accrue vs. accumulate: Accrue often describes benefits, interest, or consequences that build up over time; accumulate means to gather or collect things gradually.
- Accuracy vs. accurate: Accuracy is a noun describing correctness; accurate is an adjective describing something correct or precise.
- Accusation vs. accuse: Accusation is the claim; accuse is the act of making the claim.
- Achieve vs. achievement: Achieve is the action of accomplishing something; achievement is the result.
- Acquaint vs. acquainted vs. acquaintance: Acquaint means to make familiar; acquainted means familiar; acquaintance means a person known or knowledge of a subject.
- Acquire vs. acquisition: Acquire is a verb; acquisition is the process or result of obtaining something.
- Actual vs. actually: Actual describes what is real; actually modifies a statement to indicate what is really true.
- Additional vs. additive: Additional means extra; additive usually refers to something added to another substance or product.
- Address: You can address a person or audience by speaking to them, or address a problem by dealing with it directly.

Practice challenge: Without looking at the definitions, explain the differences between accrue and accumulate, acquaintance and acquainted, and actual and actually.

# Page 4 — Vocabulary (Adequate → Adventure)

1\. Adequate — sufficient; enough to meet a particular need or purpose.

- The company provided adequate training before assigning employees their responsibilities.
- Although his salary was not high, it was adequate to cover his basic expenses.

2\. Adhere — to stick firmly to a surface; to follow or obey a rule, principle, or agreement.

- The label will adhere to the bottle if the surface is clean and dry.
- All employees must adhere to the company's safety regulations.

3\. Adhesive — a substance used to stick or bond objects together; glue.

- The carpenter used a strong adhesive to attach the wooden pieces.
- This adhesive is designed to bond plastic surfaces permanently.

4\. Adjacent — next to or very close to something.

- Our office is adjacent to the main conference room.
- The hotel has an adjacent parking lot for its guests.

5\. Adjoin — to be directly next to or connected with another place or area.

- The kitchen and dining room adjoin each other.
- Their property adjoins a large public park.

6\. Adjust — to change something slightly to make it suitable; to adapt to new circumstances.

- You can adjust the brightness of your screen in the settings.
- It took him several weeks to adjust to living in a new country.

7\. Adjustment — a change made to improve suitability or accuracy; the process of adapting.

- The technician made an adjustment to the machine's temperature settings.
- Moving abroad required a major adjustment to her daily routine.

8\. Administer — to manage or supervise something; to give medication or treatment.

- The hospital hired a new director to administer its daily operations.
- The nurse administered the medication according to the doctor's instructions.

9\. Administration — the management of an organization; the people responsible for managing it; the process of governing.

- The administration of the university introduced new examination policies.
- The hospital administration met to discuss its annual budget.

10\. Administrative — relating to the management or organization of an institution or business.

- She handles administrative tasks such as scheduling meetings and organizing documents.
- The company introduced an administrative system to simplify employee records.

11\. Admiration — a feeling of respect, approval, or appreciation for someone or something.

- She has great admiration for scientists who dedicate their lives to research.
- His courage earned the admiration of everyone on the team.

12\. Admire — to respect, appreciate, or look at someone or something with pleasure and wonder.

- I admire people who remain honest even when honesty is difficult.
- Tourists gathered to admire the beautiful architecture of the historic building.

13\. Admission — permission to enter; the act of acknowledging or confessing something.

- Admission to the museum is free on Fridays.
- His admission that he had made a mistake helped resolve the disagreement.

14\. Admit — to allow someone to enter; to acknowledge or confess something.

- The security guard refused to admit anyone without a valid identification card.
- She admitted that she had misunderstood the instructions.

15\. Admittedly — as is acknowledged or accepted; used to concede a point.

- Admittedly, the first version of the application had several serious weaknesses.
- The journey was admittedly expensive, but the experience was worth it.

16\. Adolescence — the transitional period between childhood and adulthood.

- During adolescence, many people begin to develop a stronger sense of identity.
- His interest in computer science began during adolescence.

17\. Adolescent — a young person who is developing from a child into an adult; relating to adolescence.

- The program provides psychological support for adolescents experiencing academic pressure.
- Adolescent behavior can be influenced by family, friends, and social environments.

18\. Adopt — to legally take a child into one's family; to accept or begin using an idea, method, or practice.

- The couple decided to adopt a child after years of consideration.
- The company plans to adopt artificial intelligence tools to improve productivity.

19\. Adoption — the legal process of taking a child into a family; the acceptance or introduction of an idea or practice.

- The adoption of the child gave him a permanent and loving home.
- The adoption of cloud computing reduced the company's infrastructure costs.

20\. Adore — to love someone deeply; to regard someone or something with intense affection or admiration.

- She adores her younger brother and always looks after him.
- Many fans adore the singer for her voice and personality.

21\. Adorn — to decorate or make something more attractive.

- They used flowers to adorn the entrance to the wedding hall.
- The walls were adorned with paintings by local artists.

22\. Adornment — a decoration or ornament; something that makes a person or object more attractive.

- The necklace was a beautiful adornment for her evening dress.
- The building's elaborate stone adornments reflected the artistic style of the period.

23\. Adult — a fully grown person; someone who has reached legal adulthood.

- Every adult must take responsibility for their own decisions.
- The course is designed for adult learners who want to improve their digital skills.

24\. Advance — to move forward; to make progress; to develop or promote something.

- The soldiers continued to advance toward the border.
- She worked hard to advance her career in software engineering.

25\. Advanced — highly developed, sophisticated, or beyond a basic level.

- The university offers an advanced course in machine learning.
- The laboratory uses advanced equipment to conduct scientific experiments.

26\. Adventure — an unusual or exciting experience involving uncertainty, risk, or discovery.

- Traveling alone through unfamiliar cities was an exciting adventure.
- Starting a business without knowing whether it would succeed was a risky adventure.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- Adequate vs. sufficient: Both mean enough for a purpose. Adequate often means acceptable or meeting minimum requirements.
- Adhere vs. adhesive: Adhere is a verb meaning to stick or follow; adhesive is a substance that makes things stick.
- Adjacent vs. adjoin: Adjacent describes things that are next to each other; adjoin describes the relationship of being directly next to or connected with something.
- Adjust vs. adjustment: Adjust is the action; adjustment is the change or process.
- Administer vs. administration vs. administrative: To manage or give something; the management or managing body; relating to management.
- Admiration vs. admire: Admiration is the feeling; admire is the action of appreciating or respecting.
- Admission vs. admit: Admission is permission to enter or an acknowledgment; admit means to allow entry or confess.
- Adopt vs. adapt: Adopt means accept or take on; adapt means change to suit new conditions.
- Adorn vs. adornment: Adorn means decorate; adornment is the decoration itself.
- Advance vs. advanced: Advance can mean moving forward or making progress; advanced describes something highly developed or at a higher level.

Practice challenge: Explain the difference between adopt and adapt, then create one sentence using admittedly and another using admission in the sense of confessing something.

# Page 5 — Vocabulary (Adventurous → Agreeable)

1\. Adventurous — willing to take risks or try unfamiliar experiences; daring and venturesome.

- She is adventurous enough to travel alone through unfamiliar countries.
- His adventurous spirit led him to start a business despite the uncertainty.

2\. Adversary — an opponent or enemy who competes or fights against someone.

- The chess player carefully studied his adversary's strategy before the match.
- In the courtroom, the lawyer presented strong arguments against her adversary.

3\. Adverse — harmful, unfavorable, or opposing a desired outcome.

- The project was delayed because of adverse weather conditions.
- The medication may cause adverse effects in some patients.

4\. Adversity — a difficult or unfortunate situation; hardship or misfortune.

- She remained determined despite the adversity she faced after losing her job.
- Adversity taught him to become more patient and independent.

5\. Advertise — to promote something publicly; to make information about a product, service, or opportunity known.

- The company plans to advertise its new application on social media.
- The university advertised several scholarships for international students.

6\. Advisable — sensible, wise, or recommended under particular circumstances.

- It is advisable to back up your files before updating the operating system.
- Given the financial risks, it would be advisable to postpone the purchase.

7\. Advocate — to publicly support or recommend an idea, policy, or cause; to argue in favor of something.

- She advocates equal access to education for all children.
- The engineer advocated replacing the outdated system with a more secure solution.

8\. Aerial — relating to the air or atmosphere; taking place in the air.

- The rescue team used an aerial photograph to locate the missing hikers.
- The performers amazed the audience with their aerial acrobatics.

9\. Affect — to influence or produce a change in someone's feelings, thoughts, or circumstances.

- Lack of sleep can affect your concentration and memory.
- The new regulations will affect how small businesses operate.

10\. Affection — a feeling of fondness, warmth, or love toward someone.

- She showed great affection for her younger sister.
- His affection for animals inspired him to volunteer at a shelter.

11\. Affectionate — showing warmth, love, or tenderness toward someone.

- The affectionate father hugged his daughter before she left for school.
- My cat is particularly affectionate when I return home after a long day.

12\. Affiliate — to associate or connect an organization or person with another; to formally join a group or institution.

- The small research center decided to affiliate with a major university.
- She became affiliated with a professional association for data scientists.

13\. Affirm — to state firmly that something is true; to confirm or declare support for something.

- The witness affirmed that he had seen the accident.
- The manager affirmed her commitment to improving working conditions.

14\. Affirmation — a positive statement or confirmation; a formal declaration of something believed to be true.

- Receiving affirmation from her supervisor increased her confidence.
- The court issued an affirmation of the earlier decision.

15\. Affirmative — expressing agreement, approval, or confirmation; positive rather than negative.

- When asked whether he accepted the offer, he gave an affirmative answer.
- The committee reached an affirmative decision on the proposed changes.

16\. Afford — to have enough money or resources for something; to be able to bear a cost or sacrifice.

- I cannot afford a new laptop until I have saved enough money.
- We cannot afford to waste time during the final stage of the project.

17\. Affordable — reasonably priced or within someone's financial means.

- The university offers affordable accommodation for international students.
- They are searching for an affordable laptop suitable for programming.

18\. Agency — an organization that provides a particular service; a government department or administrative body.

- She hired a marketing agency to promote her online business.
- The environmental protection agency introduced new rules to reduce pollution.

19\. Agent — a person or organization authorized to act on behalf of another.

- The travel agent arranged our flights and hotel reservations.
- The company appointed an agent to negotiate contracts with foreign suppliers.

20\. Aggravate — to make a problem, condition, or situation worse; to irritate or annoy someone.

- Driving with a damaged engine may aggravate the mechanical problem.
- His sarcastic comments only aggravated the disagreement between the colleagues.

21\. Aggregate — to gather separate items into a whole; to combine amounts into a total.

- The software can aggregate sales data from several different branches.
- The researchers aggregated the survey responses to calculate the overall result.

22\. Aggregation — the process or result of collecting separate items into a group or whole.

- The aggregation of customer feedback helped the company identify common complaints.
- Data aggregation allowed the analysts to compare sales across multiple regions.

23\. Aggressive — forceful, hostile, or ready to attack; pursuing goals with great intensity.

- The aggressive driver repeatedly honked and refused to let other cars pass.
- The company adopted an aggressive marketing strategy to increase its market share.

24\. Aggressor — a person, group, or country that initiates an attack or acts aggressively.

- The soldiers were ordered to defend the village against the aggressor.
- Investigators identified the aggressor who started the physical confrontation.

25\. Agitate — to disturb or upset someone; to stir or shake something vigorously.

- The announcement agitated employees who feared losing their jobs.
- Agitate the mixture thoroughly before pouring it into the container.

26\. Agitation — a state of anxiety, nervous excitement, or emotional disturbance; the act of stirring something.

- His agitation became obvious as he waited for the interview results.
- The chemical solution requires constant agitation to keep its ingredients evenly mixed.

27\. Agony — extreme physical pain or intense emotional suffering.

- The injured athlete was in agony after falling heavily on the field.
- She experienced emotional agony when her closest friend moved abroad.

28\. Agreeable — pleasant or acceptable; willing to agree or cooperate.

- We spent an agreeable afternoon talking about books and traveling.
- The client found the proposed payment schedule agreeable and signed the contract.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- Adversary vs. adverse vs. adversity: An adversary is an opponent; adverse describes something unfavorable; adversity means hardship.
- Affect vs. affection: Affect is usually a verb meaning influence; affection is a noun meaning fondness or love.
- Affirm vs. affirmation vs. affirmative: To confirm; a confirmation or positive statement; expressing agreement or confirmation.
- Afford vs. affordable: Afford means having the resources to pay for or do something; affordable describes something within one's financial means.
- Agency vs. agent: An agency is an organization; an agent is a person or entity acting on behalf of another.
- Aggravate vs. agitate: Aggravate makes a problem worse; agitate disturbs someone emotionally or stirs something physically.
- Aggregate vs. aggregation: Aggregate means to combine into a whole; aggregation is the process or result of combining.
- Aggressive vs. aggressor: Aggressive describes behavior; an aggressor is the person or group initiating an attack.
- Agreeable vs. advisable: Agreeable means acceptable or pleasant; advisable means sensible or recommended.

Practice challenge: Complete these sentences from memory:

1. It is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ to save a copy of your work before making major changes.
2. The unexpected expenses \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ the company's financial difficulties.
3. The analyst used data \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ to combine information from different sources.
4. Despite facing \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_, she continued working toward her goal.
5. I cannot \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ to buy an expensive computer right now.

# Page 6 — Vocabulary (Agreement → Along)

1\. Agreement — a state of sharing the same opinion; a formal arrangement or contract between parties.

- After a long discussion, the two managers reached an agreement about the project budget.
- Their agreement on how to raise the children helped maintain harmony in the family.

2\. Agriculture — the practice of cultivating land, growing crops, and raising animals for food or other products.

- Agriculture remains an important source of income for many rural communities.
- Modern agriculture uses technology to improve crop production and reduce water consumption.

3\. Aid — help or assistance; a person or thing that makes a task easier.

- The government provided financial aid to families affected by the flood.
- A calculator is a useful aid when solving complicated mathematical problems.

4\. Ailment — an illness or health problem, usually one that is not extremely serious.

- She visited the doctor because of a persistent stomach ailment.
- Regular exercise may help prevent certain common ailments.

5\. Aim — to point or direct something toward a target; to intend or plan to achieve something.

- The archer took a deep breath and aimed at the center of the target.
- My aim is to become a skilled AI engineer within the next few years.

6\. Air — to broadcast a program on television or radio; to express or make something known publicly.

- The television station will air a documentary about climate change tonight.
- The employees aired their concerns during the meeting.

7\. Aircraft — a vehicle designed to travel through the air, such as an airplane or helicopter.

- The aircraft landed safely despite the strong winds.
- Engineers are developing more fuel-efficient aircraft to reduce emissions.

8\. Aisle — a passage between rows of seats, shelves, or other objects.

- The flight attendant walked down the aisle to check the passengers' seat belts.
- You can find the cleaning products in the aisle next to the laundry supplies.

9\. Ajar — slightly open, usually referring to a door, window, or other opening.

- I noticed that the front door was ajar when I returned home.
- She left the window ajar to let some fresh air into the room.

10\. Alarm — a warning signal or sound indicating danger; a feeling of sudden fear or concern.

- The fire alarm rang throughout the building, forcing everyone to evacuate.
- The sudden drop in sales caused alarm among the company's investors.

11\. Album — a collection of photographs, recordings, or other related items; a book used to store or display them.

- My grandmother showed me an album filled with photographs from her childhood.
- The band released a new album containing twelve original songs.

12\. Alert — a warning about danger; watchful and ready to respond to a situation.

- The weather service issued an alert about the approaching storm.
- Drivers must remain alert when traveling on crowded roads.

13\. Alien — unfamiliar or foreign; very different from one's usual surroundings, beliefs, or experiences.

- The customs and traditions of the unfamiliar society initially seemed alien to him.
- The idea of working without internet access felt completely alien to the software developer.

14\. Alienate — to make someone feel isolated or unfriendly toward you; to transfer ownership of property.

- Constantly criticizing his colleagues began to alienate him from the rest of the team.
- The owner decided to alienate the property by transferring it to another party.

15\. Alignment — an arrangement in a straight line or appropriate position; agreement or cooperation between people, goals, or organizations.

- The technician checked the alignment of the printer's moving parts.
- The company's new strategy requires alignment between its departments and long-term objectives.

16\. Alike — similar to each other; in the same way or to the same degree.

- The two brothers look remarkably alike despite having different personalities.
- The new policy affects experienced employees and beginners alike.

17\. Allergic — having an immune-system reaction to a particular substance; extremely sensitive or opposed to something in figurative use.

- She is allergic to peanuts and must carefully check food labels.
- He seems allergic to criticism and becomes defensive whenever someone offers feedback.

18\. Allergy — an immune-system reaction to a substance that is usually harmless to most people.

- His allergy to pollen causes sneezing during the spring.
- The restaurant asks customers to report any food allergy before ordering.

19\. Alliance — a partnership or union formed for mutual benefit, protection, or a shared objective.

- The two countries formed an alliance to improve regional security.
- The technology companies established an alliance to develop a shared software platform.

20\. Allocate — to distribute or assign resources, money, time, or responsibilities according to a plan.

- The manager allocated additional funds to the software development team.
- You should allocate enough time for revision before your examination.

21\. Allot — to give or distribute a specific portion or share to someone.

- Each student was allotted twenty minutes to complete the presentation.
- The committee allotted a portion of its budget to employee training.

22\. Allowance — an amount of money or another resource given regularly or permitted for a particular purpose; permission or consideration granted in certain contexts.

- His parents give him a monthly allowance to cover personal expenses.
- The budget includes an allowance for unexpected maintenance costs.

23\. Alloy — a mixture of two or more elements, at least one of which is a metal; to mix substances to form such a material.

- Brass is an alloy made primarily of copper and zinc.
- Engineers use an alloy of metals to improve the strength of certain machine components.

24\. Ally — a person, group, or country that cooperates with another toward a common goal.

- The small business found an ally in a larger company that supported its expansion.
- During the conflict, the two countries worked closely with their allies.

25\. Alone — without other people; by oneself.

- She prefers to study alone because it helps her concentrate.
- Although he lived alone, he regularly met his friends and family.

26\. Along — moving forward or following the length of something; accompanying someone or progressing together.

- We walked along the river until we reached the old bridge.
- My colleague came along with me to the conference.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- Agreement vs. alliance: An agreement is a shared decision or arrangement; an alliance is a partnership formed to pursue common interests.
- Aid vs. allowance: Aid provides help or support; an allowance is a permitted or allocated amount of money or resources.
- Aim vs. air: Aim means direct toward a target or intend to achieve something; air can mean broadcast or express something publicly.
- Alarm vs. alert: An alarm signals danger or expresses fear; an alert is a warning or a state of watchfulness.
- Ailment vs. allergy: An ailment is an illness or health problem; an allergy is a specific immune reaction to a substance.
- Allocate vs. allot: Both mean distribute, but allocate often refers to assigning resources according to a plan, while allot emphasizes giving someone a specific share.
- Alike vs. alone: Alike means similar; alone means by oneself.
- Ally vs. alliance: An ally is a partner; an alliance is the partnership itself.
- Alien vs. alienate: Alien describes something foreign or unfamiliar; alienate means to make someone feel isolated or to transfer ownership.
- Along vs. alike: Along often describes direction or movement; alike describes similarity.

Practice challenge: Fill in the blanks without looking back.

1. The manager decided to \_\_\_\_\_\_ more money to employee training.
2. Both candidates were treated \_\_\_\_\_\_ during the selection process.
3. The door was left \_\_\_\_\_\_, so I pushed it open.
4. Poor communication can \_\_\_\_\_\_ employees from their managers.
5. The two companies formed an \_\_\_\_\_\_ to develop new technology.
6. She walked \_\_\_\_\_\_ the beach while listening to music.
