# PAGE 52

**doll** — A toy made to look like a person, usually a child.

- The little girl played with her favorite doll.
- He bought a doll as a birthday present.

**domain** — An area of knowledge, activity, or control; also, an internet address category.

- Medicine is a specialized domain of knowledge.
- The company registered a new internet domain.

**dome** — A rounded roof or structure shaped like half a sphere.

- The building has a large glass dome.
- We visited a mosque with a beautiful dome.

**domestic** — Relating to the home or family; also, relating to one's own country.

- She prefers domestic travel.
- Domestic animals are often kept as pets or farm animals.

**dominant** — More powerful, influential, or common than others.

- English is a dominant language in many international workplaces.
- The dominant company controls most of the market.

**dominate** — To control or have a strong influence over someone or something.

- One company dominates the local market.
- He tends to dominate every conversation.

**donate** — To give money, goods, or time to help a person or organization.

- Many people donate clothes to charity.
- She decided to donate blood.

**donation** — Money, goods, or time given to help someone or an organization.

- The hospital received a large donation.
- Every donation helps support the charity.

**doom** — A terrible fate or destruction; to cause something to fail or end badly.

- The failure of the project seemed like certain doom.
- Poor planning could doom the business to failure.

**dormitory** — A building or room where students or other people sleep.

- The university provides a dormitory for students.
- She shares a dormitory room with two classmates.

**dose** — A measured amount of medicine or another substance.

- Take one dose of the medicine after dinner.
- The doctor reduced the daily dose.

**dot** — A very small round mark or spot.

- There is a red dot on the screen.
- Connect the dots to complete the picture.

**doubt** — A feeling of uncertainty or lack of confidence.

- I have some doubt about the accuracy of this information.
- There is no doubt that practice improves your skills.

**doubtful** — Uncertain or unlikely to happen.

- It is doubtful that the meeting will finish early.
- She was doubtful about the success of the plan.

**doubtless** — Almost certainly; very probably.

- He will doubtless arrive before noon.
- This is doubtless one of the best solutions available.

**downtown** — The central business or commercial area of a city.

- We went downtown to do some shopping.
- Her office is located downtown.

**doze** — To sleep lightly for a short period.

- He began to doze during the lecture.
- I dozed on the sofa after lunch.

**draft** — An early version of a piece of writing; also, a current of air or a process of selecting people for military service.

- I finished the first draft of my report.
- There is a cold draft coming through the window.

**drag** — To pull something along a surface; to move slowly or unwillingly.

- He dragged the heavy suitcase upstairs.
- The meeting seemed to drag on forever.

**drain** — To remove liquid from something; to use up energy or resources.

- Drain the water from the pasta.
- The long journey drained all my energy.

**drainage** — The system or process of removing excess water.

- The road needs better drainage.
- Poor drainage caused flooding in the garden.

**drama** — A play or performance; also, an exciting or emotional situation.

- We watched a drama at the theater.
- There was unnecessary drama during the meeting.

**dramatic** — Very noticeable, sudden, or exciting; relating to drama.

- The company experienced a dramatic increase in sales.
- She gave a dramatic performance.

**dramatize** — To present something as a drama or make it seem more exciting or serious than it is.

- The film dramatizes an important historical event.
- He tends to dramatize small problems.

**drastic** — Very extreme and having a strong effect.

- The company needs to take drastic measures to reduce costs.
- There was a drastic change in the weather.

**draw** — To make a picture; to pull something; to attract attention; or to finish a game without a winner.

- She likes to draw portraits.
- The advertisement helped draw new customers.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- **Domain** = an area of knowledge or control; **dome** = a rounded roof.
- **Donate** = give something; **donation** = what is given.
- **Doubtful** = uncertain; **doubtless** = almost certainly.
- **Draft** = an early version or a current of air; **drastic** = extreme.
- **Drain** = remove liquid or energy; **drainage** = the system or process of removing water.
- **Drama** = a play or emotional situation; **dramatic** = striking or theatrical; **dramatize** = present as drama or exaggerate.

---

# PAGE 53

**drawback** — A disadvantage or negative feature of something.

- The main drawback of this phone is its short battery life.
- Living far from work has several drawbacks.

**drawer** — A sliding box in a desk, cabinet, or other piece of furniture used for storage.

- I keep my documents in the top drawer.
- She opened the drawer to find a pen.

**drawing** — A picture made with a pencil, pen, or other tool; also, the act of creating such a picture.

- The child made a drawing of her house.
- His technical drawing shows the machine's design.

**dread** — A strong feeling of fear or worry about something that may happen.

- She felt dread before the difficult examination.
- Many people dread speaking in public.

**dreadful** — Extremely bad, unpleasant, or frightening.

- We had dreadful weather during the trip.
- The food tasted dreadful.

**dreary** — Dull, gloomy, and lacking interest or excitement.

- It was a dreary afternoon with constant rain.
- The office looked dreary and poorly maintained.

**drench** — To make something completely wet.

- The sudden rain drenched everyone waiting outside.
- He was drenched after walking home in the storm.

**drift** — To move slowly without control, usually because of wind or water; to gradually move away from a subject or direction.

- The boat began to drift toward the shore.
- Our conversation drifted toward our childhood memories.

**drill** — A tool used to make holes; also, repeated practice or training.

- He used a drill to make a hole in the wall.
- The students completed a fire drill.

**drip** — To fall or let liquid fall in small drops.

- Water began to drip from the ceiling.
- Be careful not to drip coffee on the table.

**drought** — A long period with very little or no rain.

- The drought destroyed many crops.
- Farmers struggled to find water during the drought.

**drown** — To die or be killed by being unable to breathe underwater; also, to overwhelm or cover a sound.

- The lifeguard rescued the swimmer before he could drown.
- The loud music drowned out our conversation.

**dubious** — Doubtful, uncertain, or not completely trustworthy.

- The offer sounded too good to be true and seemed dubious.
- The company has a dubious reputation.

**due** — Expected or scheduled to happen; owed or required.

- The assignment is due on Monday.
- The payment is due at the end of the month.

**dull** — Not sharp, bright, interesting, or exciting; also, a mild and continuous type of pain.

- The knife is too dull to cut the vegetables.
- The lecture was dull, and several students became bored.

**duly** — In the proper or expected way; at the appropriate time.

- The application was duly signed and submitted.
- The guests were duly informed of the changes.

**dumb** — Informally, stupid or lacking good judgment; historically used to mean unable to speak, but that usage is outdated and can be offensive.

- It was a dumb mistake that could have been avoided.
- I felt dumb after forgetting the appointment.

**dump** — To throw something away carelessly; to unload or leave something or someone.

- Please do not dump rubbish in the street.
- He dumped the old furniture outside the house.

**duplicate** — An exact copy of something; to make an exact copy.

- Please make a duplicate of this key.
- The system detected a duplicate entry.

**durable** — Strong and able to last a long time without being damaged.

- These shoes are durable enough for daily use.
- We need durable materials for the construction project.

**duration** — The length of time that something continues.

- The duration of the meeting was two hours.
- The course has a duration of six months.

**dwarf** — To make something appear very small in comparison; also, a type of small star, plant, or animal.

- The huge building dwarfed the houses around it.
- Astronomers discovered a distant dwarf star.

**dwell** — To live in a particular place; "dwell on" means to think or talk about something repeatedly.

- Few people dwell in this remote area.
- Try not to dwell on your past mistakes.

**dweller** — A person or animal that lives in a particular place.

- The city has millions of urban dwellers.
- The desert dweller has adapted to extreme conditions.

**dwelling** — A place where someone lives, such as a house or apartment.

- The family lives in a small dwelling near the river.
- The building contains several separate dwellings.

**dye** — A substance used to change the color of something; to color something using such a substance.

- She bought a blue dye for the fabric.
- He decided to dye his hair brown.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- **Drawback** = disadvantage; **dread** = strong fear or worry.
- **Dreadful** = extremely bad; **dreary** = dull and gloomy.
- **Drift** = move slowly without control; **drip** = fall in small drops.
- **Drill** = a tool or repeated practice; **drain** = remove liquid or energy.
- **Dubious** = doubtful or suspicious; **doubtless** = almost certainly.
- **Dull** = boring or not sharp; **duly** = properly or as expected.
- **Drawer** = a storage compartment; **drawing** = a picture or the act of making one.
- **Dye** = color something; **drown** = die or be killed by submersion.

**drench** — To make something completely wet.

- The rain drenched our clothes.
- The workers were drenched in sweat after the long shift.

- The discussion drifted away from the main topic.

- He used an electric drill to repair the shelf.
- The school organized a fire drill.

**drip** — To fall or let liquid fall in small drops.

- Water dripped from the broken pipe.
- The wet umbrella continued to drip on the floor.

**drought** — A long period of unusually low rainfall.

- The drought affected farmers across the region.
- The government introduced measures to deal with the drought.

**drown** — To die or be killed by being unable to breathe underwater; also, to overwhelm or cover a sound.

- The swimmer nearly drowned in the deep water.
- The traffic noise drowned out the conversation.

**dubious** — Doubtful, questionable, or not completely trustworthy.

- He gave a dubious explanation for his absence.
- The website makes several dubious claims.

**due** — Expected or scheduled to happen; owed or required.

- The train is due to arrive at six.
- The rent is due tomorrow.

**dull** — Not sharp, bright, exciting, or interesting; also, a mild continuous pain.

- This knife is dull and needs sharpening.
- I have a dull pain in my back.

**duly** — Properly or at the expected time.

- The documents were duly delivered.
- The invitation was duly accepted.

**dumb** — Informally, stupid or lacking good judgment; historically used to mean unable to speak, but that meaning is outdated and can be offensive.

- That was a dumb decision.
- I felt dumb when I realized I had read the instructions incorrectly.

**dump** — To throw something away carelessly; to unload or abandon something.

- People should not dump rubbish in public places.
- They dumped the old computer because it no longer worked.

**duplicate** — An exact copy of something; to make an exact copy.

- The office keeps a duplicate of every important document.
- Please duplicate this file before editing it.

**durable** — Strong and able to last for a long time.

- This material is durable and easy to clean.
- We need durable equipment for the factory.

**duration** — The length of time that something lasts.

- The duration of the film is two hours.
- The course has a duration of twelve weeks.

**dwarf** — To make something appear small in comparison; also, a type of small star, plant, or animal.

- The skyscraper dwarfed the surrounding buildings.
- A white dwarf is a small, dense star.

**dwell** — To live in a place; "dwell on" means to think or speak about something repeatedly.

- Many families dwell in this rural area.
- Do not dwell on things you cannot change.

**dweller** — A person or animal that lives in a particular place.

- The city dweller enjoys access to many services.
- Desert dwellers have developed ways to survive in dry environments.

**dwelling** — A place where people live, such as a house or apartment.

- The family built a small dwelling near the farm.
- The old dwelling was renovated last year.

**dye** — A substance used to change the color of something; to color something with such a substance.

- The fabric needs a darker dye.
- She decided to dye her hair red.

## Quick distinctions to remember

- **Dread** = fear or worry; **dreadful** = extremely bad; **dreary** = gloomy and dull.
- **Drill** = a tool or training exercise; **drip** = liquid falling in drops.
- **Drought** = a long period without enough rain; **drown** = die or be killed underwater.
- **Dubious** = questionable; **due** = expected or owed; **duly** = properly.
- **Durable** = long-lasting; **duration** = length of time.
- **Drawer** = storage compartment; **drawing** = picture.
- **Dye** = color something; **dwell** = live somewhere or think repeatedly about something.

- - The invention marked a new epoch in technology.  
- - We are living in an important epoch of scientific development.  

**equal** — the same in amount, value, or status  
- - All people should have equal opportunities.  
- - Divide the money into equal parts.  

**equality** — the state of being equal  
- - The organization promotes equality in education.  
- - Everyone deserves dignity and equality.  

**equation** — a mathematical statement showing two things are equal  
- - Solve the equation carefully.  
- - The teacher explained the equation step by step.  

**equator** — an imaginary line around the middle of the Earth  
- - Countries near the equator usually have warm climates.  
- - The equator divides the Earth into two hemispheres.  

**equilibrium** — a state of balance  
- - She tried to maintain a balance between work and life.  
- - The system reached an equilibrium.  

**equip** — provide with necessary tools or skills  
- - The course equips students with practical skills.  
- - We need to equip the laboratory with new computers.  

**equipment** — tools or machines needed for a task  
- - The laboratory has modern equipment.  
- - Safety equipment is required on the site.  

**equivalent** — equal in value, meaning, or importance  
- - One kilometer is equivalent to one thousand meters.  
- - The two qualifications are roughly equivalent.  

**eradicate** — completely destroy or remove  
- - The campaign aims to eradicate the disease.  
- - Education can help eradicate illiteracy.  

**erase** — remove writing or information  
- - Please erase the old information.  
- - He erased the mistake from the board.  

**erect** — build or put something upright  
- - Workers erected a new building.  
- - They erected a monument in the park.  

**erosion** — the gradual wearing away of material  
- - Heavy rain caused soil erosion.  
- - Coastal erosion threatens many buildings.  

**errand** — a short trip to complete a task  
- - I need to run an errand before work.  
- - She went to the store on an errand.  

**erupt** — suddenly burst out  
- - The volcano may erupt again.  
- - Violence erupted after the event.  

**eruption** — a sudden violent outburst  
- - The volcanic eruption covered the area with ash.  
- - The eruption forced many people to leave their homes.  

**escalator** — a moving staircase  
- - We took the escalator to the next floor.  
- - The escalator stopped suddenly.  

**escape** — get away from danger or confinement  
- - The prisoners attempted to escape.  
- - He escaped from the burning building.  

**escort** — accompany someone for protection or guidance  
- - A guard escorted the visitors inside.  
- - She was escorted to the airport.  

**essay** — a short piece of writing about a subject  
- - I need to write an essay about technology.  
- - Her essay received a high grade.  

**essence** — the most important quality of something  
- - The essence of the problem is easy to understand.  
- - Trust is the essence of a strong relationship.  

**essential** — completely necessary  
- - Water is essential for life.  
- - Communication is an essential skill.

# PAGE 61

**establish** — set up or create  
- - They established a new company.  
- - The research established a connection between the two factors.  

**establishment** — an organization or the act of creating something  
- - The establishment of the university took many years.  
- - The restaurant is a popular local establishment.  

**estate** — property or all the assets owned by someone  
- - He inherited a large estate.  
- - The family owns an estate outside the city.  

**esteem** — respect and admire  
- - She is held in high esteem by her colleagues.  
- - I esteem him for his honesty.  

**estimate** — roughly calculate or judge the value of something  
- - We estimate that the project will take six months.  
- - The engineer estimated the cost of the project.  

**eternal** — lasting forever  
- - The story explores the idea of eternal life.  
- - Some questions seem eternal.  

**evaluate** — examine and judge carefully  
- - We need to evaluate the results.  
- - The teacher evaluated the students' work.  

**evaporate** — change from liquid into vapor  
- - Water evaporates when it is heated.  
- - The rainwater quickly evaporated in the sun.  

**evaporation** — the process of changing from liquid into vapor  
- - Heat increases the rate of evaporation.  
- - Evaporation is part of the water cycle.  

**eventful** — full of important or interesting events  
- - It was an eventful year.  
- - We had an eventful journey.  

**eventually** — finally; after some time  
- - He eventually found a solution.  
- - The project eventually became successful.  

**everlasting** — lasting forever or for a very long time  
- - They hoped for everlasting peace.  
- - The monument is a symbol of everlasting memory.  

**evidence** — facts or information showing that something is true  
- - The police found evidence at the scene.  
- - There is strong evidence supporting the theory.  

**evident** — clear and easy to see or understand  
- - It was evident that she was tired.  
- - The benefits of the new system are evident.  

**evolution** — gradual development or change  
- - Technology has undergone rapid evolution.  
- - The theory explains the evolution of species.  

**evolve** — develop gradually  
- - Languages evolve over time.  
- - The project evolved into something much larger.  

**exact** — completely correct and precise  
- - Please give me the exact address.  
- - The measurements must be exact.  

**exaggerate** — make something seem larger or more important than it is  
- - Do not exaggerate the problem.  
- - He tends to exaggerate his achievements.  

**exaggeration** — a statement that makes something seem bigger than it is  
- - His story contained some exaggeration.  
- - The claim was clearly an exaggeration.  

**exalt** — raise in rank or praise highly  
- - The speech exalted the achievements of the team.  
- - Some people exalt wealth above everything else.  

**exalted** — of very high rank or importance  
- - She held an exalted position in society.  
- - He was praised for his exalted character.  

**exasperate** — make someone very annoyed  
- - The repeated delays began to exasperate us.  
- - His behavior exasperated his colleagues.  

**exceed** — go beyond a limit or amount  
- - The cost may exceed our budget.  
- - Do not exceed the speed limit.  

**exceedingly** — extremely; very  
- - The task was exceedingly difficult.  
- - She was exceedingly happy with the result.  

**excel** — be extremely good at something  
- - She excels at mathematics.  
- - Hard work can help you excel in your career.  

**excellent** — extremely good  
- - You did an excellent job.  
- - The restaurant provides excellent service.

# PAGE 62

**exception** — something that does not follow a general rule  
- - Everyone was invited with one exception.  
- - This case is an exception to the rule.  

**exceptional** — unusually excellent or different  
- - She showed exceptional talent.  
- - The team achieved exceptional results.  

**excess** — more than is necessary  
- - Excess sugar can be harmful.  
- - The company reduced excess spending.  

**excessive** — too much  
- - Excessive noise can cause stress.  
- - Avoid excessive use of your phone.  

**exchange** — give something and receive something else  
- - We exchanged ideas during the meeting.  
- - You can exchange the product within seven days.  

**excite** — make someone feel enthusiastic or interested  
- - The new opportunity excited him.  
- - Scientific discoveries excite my curiosity.  

**excitement** — a feeling of great enthusiasm  
- - There was great excitement before the event.  
- - She could not hide her excitement.  

**exciting** — causing excitement  
- - Starting a new career can be exciting.  
- - It was an exciting experience.  

**exclaim** — say something suddenly and loudly  
- - She exclaimed with surprise.  
- - "What a beautiful view!" he exclaimed.  

**exclude** — prevent from being included  
- - The list excludes temporary workers.  
- - Do not exclude anyone from the discussion.  

**exclusion** — the act of leaving someone or something out  
- - The exclusion of important data affected the results.  
- - Social exclusion can have serious consequences.  

**exclusive** — limited to a particular person or group  
- - The club offers exclusive benefits to members.  
- - This is an exclusive interview.  

**exclusively** — only; completely  
- - The product is sold exclusively online.  
- - She works exclusively on research projects.  

**excursion** — a short journey, usually for pleasure  
- - We went on an excursion to the countryside.  
- - The students enjoyed the weekend excursion.  

**excuse** — forgive or provide a reason for something  
- - Please excuse my late reply.  
- - There is no excuse for careless behavior.  

**execute** — carry out or perform  
- - The team executed the plan successfully.  
- - The computer executes instructions quickly.  

**execution** — the act of carrying out a plan or task  
- - The successful execution of the project required teamwork.  
- - Careful planning improves execution.  

**executive** — a person responsible for managing an organization  
- - She works as a senior executive.  
- - The executive made an important decision.  

**exemplify** — be a typical example of something  
- - Her work exemplifies creativity and dedication.  
- - This project exemplifies good teamwork.  

**exempt** — not required to do something  
- - Some students are exempt from the fee.  
- - He is exempt from certain requirements.  

**exert** — use effort, power, or influence  
- - The machine exerts pressure on the material.  
- - She exerted a strong influence on the team.  

**exertion** — physical or mental effort  
- - The climb required considerable exertion.  
- - After hours of exertion, he needed to rest.  

**exhaust** — use up completely  
- - The project exhausted our budget.  
- - The engine releases exhaust into the air.  

**exhausted** — extremely tired or completely used up  
- - I was exhausted after the long journey.  
- - The available resources were exhausted.  

**exhaustion** — extreme tiredness or complete depletion  
- - He stopped working because of exhaustion.  
- - Resource exhaustion can cause serious problems.  

**exhaustive** — complete and thorough  
- - The researchers conducted an exhaustive study.  
- - We need an exhaustive analysis of the data.  

**exhibit** — show or display  
- - The museum exhibits ancient artifacts.  
- - The results exhibit a clear pattern.

# **PAGE 63**

**exhibition** — a public display or presentation of something
The museum is holding an exhibition of modern art.
The exhibition attracted thousands of visitors.

**exile** — forced separation from one's home country
The writer spent many years in exile.
The government forced him into exile.

**exit** — a way out; the act of leaving
Please use the emergency exit.
We waited near the exit of the building.

**exonerate** — officially clear someone of blame or wrongdoing
The investigation exonerated the accused man.
New evidence helped exonerate the employee.

**expand** — become larger or make something larger
The company plans to expand its business.
Heat causes most metals to expand.

**expansion** — growth or an increase in size or amount
The company announced its expansion into new markets.
Population expansion can create pressure on resources.

**expedient** — convenient or useful for achieving a particular purpose, sometimes without being ideal
It was expedient to postpone the meeting.
The manager chose the most expedient solution.

**expedite** — make a process happen faster
We contacted the office to expedite the application.
Better communication can expedite the project.

**expedition** — an organized journey for a particular purpose
The scientists went on an expedition to the Arctic.
The expedition lasted several weeks.

**expel** — force someone or something to leave
The school decided to expel the student.
The machine expels hot air through a vent.

**expend** — use or spend money, time, or energy
We must expend our resources carefully.
She expended considerable effort on the research.

**expenditure** — the amount of money, time, or resources spent
Government expenditure increased this year.
We need to reduce unnecessary expenditure.

**expert** — a person with advanced knowledge or skill in a subject
She is an expert in artificial intelligence.
We asked an expert to examine the system.

**expertise** — specialized knowledge or skill
The project requires technical expertise.
He gained expertise through years of experience.

**expiration** — the end of a period of validity; the act of breathing out
Check the expiration date on the package.
The expiration of the contract caused uncertainty.

**expire** — come to an end or become invalid
My passport will expire next year.
The offer expires at midnight.

**explicit** — expressed clearly and directly
The instructions were explicit and easy to follow.
The contract contains explicit conditions.

**explode** — burst violently or suddenly
The gas tank could explode if exposed to fire.
The crowd exploded with excitement.

**exploit** — use something or someone to gain an advantage, sometimes unfairly
Some companies exploit workers by paying very low wages.
We should exploit available technology to improve efficiency.

**exploitation** — unfair use of people or resources for personal gain
The organization works to prevent worker exploitation.
The exploitation of natural resources can damage the environment.

**exploration** — the act of investigating or traveling to discover something
Space exploration requires advanced technology.
The exploration of the region revealed new resources.

**explore** — investigate or examine to discover more
Scientists explore new ways to produce clean energy.
We need to explore every possible solution.

**explorer** — someone who travels to discover unfamiliar places
The explorer crossed the desert alone.
Early explorers traveled to unknown regions.

**explosive** — capable of exploding; sudden and intense
The factory stores explosive materials safely.
The discussion became explosive after the announcement.

**export** — sell or send goods to another country
Egypt exports agricultural products to many countries.
The company plans to export its products to Europe.

**expose** — reveal or make something visible; leave something unprotected
The investigation exposed serious problems.
Do not expose the equipment to direct sunlight.

**exposition** — a detailed explanation or public exhibition
The report begins with an exposition of the main problem.
The city hosted an international technology exposition.

**exposure** — the state of being subjected to something; the act of revealing something

**exquisite** — extremely beautiful, delicate, or carefully made

**extension** — an addition or the act of making something longer

**extensive** — covering a large area or including many details

**extent** — the degree, size, or range of something

**exterior** — the outside part or appearance of something

**extinct** — no longer existing or living

**extinction** — the complete disappearance of a species

**extinguish** — put out a fire or end something

**extra** — additional; more than expected

**extraordinary** — very unusual or impressive

**extravagant** — spending or using more than necessary; excessively elaborate

**extreme** — very great or severe; at the furthest limit

**eye** — the organ used for seeing; also a small opening or a person's ability to notice details

An experienced designer has an eye for detail.

**fabric** — cloth or material made by weaving or other processes; the basic structure of something

**fabricate** — make or construct something; invent a false story

**face** — confront or deal with a situation
We must face the challenges ahead.
She faced a difficult decision.

**facilitate** — make an action or process easier

The manager helped facilitate the discussion.

**facility** — a building, service, or equipment provided for a particular purpose

**faction** — a smaller group within a larger organization with different opinions or goals

**factor** — something that influences or contributes to a result

**fade** — gradually disappear or lose color or strength
The colors began to fade in the sunlight.
His memories of that day slowly faded.

**faint** — weak or difficult to notice; briefly lose consciousness

**fairy** — an imaginary magical creature in traditional stories

**faith** — strong belief, trust, or confidence

**fall** — move downward; drop; decrease

**familiarity** — knowledge of something through experience; closeness or informality

**famine** — an extreme shortage of food affecting a large population

**fanatic** — a person with extreme and often unreasonable enthusiasm for a cause

**fancy** — imagination or a personal preference; to want or like something

**fantastic** — extremely good; extraordinary or imaginative

**fare** — the price of a journey; food provided or available

**fashion** — a popular style of clothing or behavior; a particular way of making something

**fasten** — attach or close something securely

**fatal** — causing death or disaster

**fault** — a mistake, weakness, or defect

**fee** — an amount of money paid for a service

**flush** — become red; flow suddenly and strongly
Her face flushed with embarrassment.
Water flushed through the pipes.

**flutter** — move with quick, light, irregular movements
The bird's wings fluttered rapidly.
The curtains fluttered in the breeze.

**flux** — continuous change or movement
The technology industry is constantly in flux.
The organization experienced a period of rapid flux.

**foam** — a mass of small bubbles on a liquid
Foam covered the surface of the sea.
The soap produced thick white foam.

**focus** — the main center of attention or interest; concentrate
Education is the main focus of the program.
I need to focus on completing this task.

**fodder** — food for farm animals, especially cattle or horses
The farmer stored fodder for the winter.
The animals were given fresh fodder.

**foe** — an enemy or opponent
The hero faced a powerful foe.
Fear can become your greatest foe.

**fog** — a thick cloud of tiny water droplets near the ground
Dense fog covered the road.
The airport closed because of heavy fog.

**foggy** — covered in fog; unclear or confused
It was a foggy morning.
My memory of that day is still foggy.

**fold** — bend something so that one part covers another
Fold the paper in half.
She folded the clothes and put them away.

**foliage** — the leaves of plants or trees
The garden was full of green foliage.
Autumn foliage covered the hillside.

**foolish** — lacking good judgment; unwise
It was foolish to ignore the warning.
He made a foolish decision.

**forbid** — officially or strongly refuse to allow something
The school forbids students from using phones during exams.
My parents forbade me to go out late.

**forecast** — a prediction about what will happen
The weather forecast predicts heavy rain.
Experts forecast slower economic growth.

**forefather** — an ancestor from an earlier generation
Our forefathers built the foundations of the country.
She researched the lives of her forefathers.

**foremost** — most important or leading
Safety is our foremost concern.
She is one of the foremost experts in the field.

**foresee** — predict or expect something before it happens
Nobody could foresee the sudden crisis.
We must foresee possible risks before starting.

**foretell** — predict or announce what will happen in the future
The story claims that the old woman could foretell the future.
No one can accurately foretell every economic change.

**forge** — a workshop where metal is heated and shaped; create something through effort
The blacksmith worked in a small forge.
The team forged a strong relationship over time.

**forgery** — the crime of making or using a false copy or document
The bank discovered a forgery.
He was arrested for forgery.

**forgive** — stop feeling angry or resentful toward someone
It took her years to forgive him.
You should learn to forgive yourself for past mistakes.

**formal** — official or following established rules and conventions

Employees must wear formal clothing at the ceremony.

**formation** — the arrangement or creation of something
The formation of the team took several weeks.

**former** — having held a position or existed in the past

**formerly** — in the past; previously

**formidable** — very difficult to deal with or overcome

**formula** — a set method or rule; a mathematical expression
The scientist developed a formula for the experiment.

**formulate** — create or express a plan or idea carefully

The researchers formulated a new hypothesis.

**forsake** — abandon someone or something, especially someone who depends on you

She refused to forsake her principles.

**fort** — a strong building designed for defense
The soldiers protected the old fort.
The fort stood on top of the hill.

**forthcoming** — going to happen or appear soon; willing to provide information
The forthcoming conference will focus on technology.
The manager was not forthcoming with details.

**fortitude** — courage and strength when facing difficulty
She showed great fortitude during the crisis.
It takes fortitude to continue after failure.

**fortnight** — a period of two weeks
I will return in a fortnight.
The course lasts a fortnight.

**fortress** — a large, strongly protected building or place
The army defended the fortress.
The ancient fortress overlooks the valley.

**fortunate** — lucky; having good things happen by chance
We were fortunate to find a good apartment.
She was fortunate to receive a scholarship.

**fortune** — luck; a large amount of money
He made a fortune through his business.
By good fortune, nobody was injured.

**forum** — a place or event for discussion and exchange of ideas
The university organized a forum on artificial intelligence.
Online forums allow users to share information.

**forward** — toward the front or toward the future
Please move forward slowly.
We need to plan forward for the next stage.

**fossil** — preserved remains or traces of an ancient organism
Scientists discovered a dinosaur fossil.
The museum displays fossils from millions of years ago.

**foster** — encourage the development of something; care for a child temporarily
The program aims to foster creativity.
They decided to foster a child.

**foul** — against the rules of a sport; offensive or unpleasant
The referee stopped the game because of a foul.
A foul smell came from the kitchen.

**found** — establish an organization; also, melt metal and pour it into a mold
They founded a technology company.
The workers found the metal into a new shape.

**foundation** — the base or underlying support of something
Trust is the foundation of a strong relationship.
The building has a solid concrete foundation.

**founder** — a person who establishes an organization or business
She is the founder of a technology startup.
The founder explained the company's original vision.

**fountain** — a structure that sends water into the air; a natural spring
Children played near the fountain.
Fresh water flowed from a natural fountain in the hills.

### PAGE 71

**fraction** - segment; fragment; a small proportion of

- The athlete suffered a hairline fracture in his left ankle during the final match.
- Constant disagreements threatened to fracture the long-standing partnership.

**fragile** - easy to destroy, delicate, not strong
- Please handle the antique porcelain vase carefully because it is extremely fragile.
- After months of conflict, the peace agreement between the two nations remains fragile.

**fragment** - small part broken off or detached; fraction
- Archaeologists discovered a painted pottery fragment buried beneath the temple floor.
- I could only remember a brief fragment of the dream after waking up.

**fragrance** - scent; aroma; distinctive odor that is pleasant
- The sweet fragrance of blooming jasmine filled the evening air.
- She chose a floral fragrance for her daily perfume.

**fragrant** - pleasant-smelling; odorous
- Freshly baked bread made the entire kitchen warm and fragrant.
- They walked through a fragrant garden lined with lavender and roses.

**frail** - physically weak; easily broken
- Although he looked frail after his illness, his mind remained sharp.
- The old wooden bridge was too frail to support the weight of heavy trucks.

**frame** - structure that gives shape or support; closed, often rectangular border of drawn or printed lines
- He placed the family portrait inside a handcrafted silver frame.
- Workers assembled the steel frame of the skyscraper before adding the glass panels.

**framework** - fundamental structure, as for a written work; skeleton
- The new legislation provides a legal framework for protecting digital privacy.
- She outlined the basic framework of her novel before writing the first chapter.

**franchise** - right granted by authority; right to vote; business licensed to sell a product in particular area
- He saved enough money to open a local franchise of the popular coffee chain.
- Citizens fought for decades to secure the universal franchise for all adults.

**frank** - honest; sincere; open and sincere in expression; straightforward
- I appreciate your frank feedback on my presentation, even though it was hard to hear.
- They had a frank discussion about the company's financial struggles.

**freight** - transport commercially as cargo; load with goods; goods carried by a large vehicle
- A long freight train carried coal and timber across the mountains.
- The shipping company charges by weight to transport heavy freight overseas.

**fret** - cause to be uneasy; wear away
- Do not fret over minor details that are completely out of your control.
- She tends to fret about her upcoming exams weeks in advance.

**friction** - clash in opinion; rubbing against; conflict
- Applying oil to the hinges reduces friction and stops the door from squeaking.
- Differences in management style created noticeable friction between the two co-founders.

**frightening** - inspiring with fear; causing fear; of capable of causing fear; scary
- Getting caught in a sudden thunderstorm on the open lake was a frightening experience.
- The horror movie relied on sudden loud noises rather than a genuinely frightening plot.

**fringe** - margin; periphery; decorative border of hanging threads, cords, or strips, often attached to a separate band
- She wore a suede jacket decorated with long leather fringe along the sleeves.
- Several small cottages sat on the quiet fringe of the dense forest.

**frontier** - part of a country which fronts or faces another country or an unsettled region; border, confine, or extreme part of a country
- Pioneers traveled west to settle the vast, untamed frontier.
- Deep-space exploration represents the next great frontier for modern science.

**frost** - ice crystal; weather cold enough to cause freezing; provide with a rough surface or appearance
- A thin layer of white frost covered the grass early in the morning.
- The sudden autumn frost damaged most of the late-blooming tomatoes.

**frown** - look angry; wrinkle one's forehead; regard something with disapproval or distaste
- The teacher gave a slight frown when the students continued talking during the lecture.
- Many traditional critics frown upon mixing formal and casual attire.

**frugal** - sparing; economical; costing little; inexpensive
- By cooking at home and avoiding impulse buys, he maintained a very frugal lifestyle.
- She prepared a delicious yet frugal meal using simple pantry staples.

**fruitful** - productive; fertile; producing results; profitable

**frustrate** - make null; bring to nothing; prevent from taking effect or attaining fulfillment

**frustration** - disappointment; failure; act of frustrating

- Software glitches are a common source of daily frustration for office workers.

**fuel** - substance that can be consumed to produce energy
- The hikers gathered dry branches to use as fuel for their campfire.
- Rising fuel prices have increased the cost of cross-country shipping.

**fulfil** - fill full; fill to the utmost capacity, as a vessel, a room
- She worked tirelessly to fulfil her lifelong dream of becoming a surgeon.
- The vendor failed to fulfil the terms of the contract on time.

**full** - filled to satisfaction with food or drink; containing as much or as many as is possible or normal
- I could not eat another bite because I am completely full from dinner.
- The parking lot was already full an hour before the concert started.

- She used needle and thread to mend the torn seam on her favorite jacket.
- Honest apologies go a long way toward mending strained friendships.

**mental** - involving mind or intellectual process; affected by disorder of mind
- Solving logic puzzles provides great mental exercise during study breaks.
- Protecting your mental energy requires setting clear boundaries around notifications.

**mentality** - mental action or power; intellectual activity; intellectuality
- A growth mentality turns debugging setbacks into learning opportunities.
- Short-term thinking is a common mentality that undermines sustainable engineering.

**mention** - speak or notice of anything, usually in a brief or cursory manner
- Did she mention what time the afternoon workshop begins?
- Be sure to mention all external dependencies in the project README.

**menu** - an agenda of things to do
- Click the settings icon in the top corner to open the configuration menu.
- The neighborhood bistro updates its seasonal menu every month.

**mercantile** - trading; commercial; of or relating to trade or traders
- Historic port cities grew wealthy as bustling centers of mercantile trade.
- The museum occupies a restored nineteenth-century mercantile warehouse.

**merchandise** - objects for sale; goods
- Store staff arranged seasonal merchandise neatly along the front display tables.
- Online retailers inspect returned merchandise before restocking items.

**merchant** - one who traffics on a large scale, especially with foreign countries; trafficker; trader
- Spice merchants traveled thousands of miles along ancient overland routes.
- Local merchants rely on foot traffic from the weekend farmers market.

**mercy** - compassionate treatment of unfortunate and helpless; willingness to spare or to help; disposition to be kind
- Small coastal boats are at the mercy of sudden squalls when far from shore.
- The judge showed mercy by reducing the penalty for the first-time offense.

**mere** - being nothing more than what is specified; considered apart from anything else; small; slight
- Even a mere five minutes of daily review keeps vocabulary fresh.
- The mere thought of losing unsaved work reminded him to commit his code.

**merge** - combine; unite
- Two lanes merge into one just before the highway bridge.
- Review all automated checks before you merge a feature branch into main.

**merit** - virtue; admirable quality or attribute; credit
- Each proposal will be evaluated strictly on its technical merit.
- Her suggestion has plenty of merit and deserves a small prototype test.

**mermaid** - legendary sea creature having the head and upper body of a woman and the tail of a fish
- Sailors told ancient tales of mermaids singing near rocky ocean reefs.
- A bronze statue of a mermaid overlooks the harbor entrance.

**mesh** - opening or space inclosed by the threads of a net between knot and knot; network or net
- Fine wire mesh on the window screen keeps mosquitoes out while letting air flow.
- Their complementary skill sets mesh together seamlessly on complex builds.

**mess** - soft semi liquid food; a quantity of food set on a table at one time; provision of food for one meal
- Clean up the kitchen mess right after cooking so dried sauces do not stick.
- Untested hotfixes can turn a clean repository into a tangled mess.

**metallic** - of or pertaining to metal; of metal nature; resembling metal
- The laptop features a sleek, brushed metallic finish that resists fingerprints.
- A faint metallic taste in tap water can indicate older plumbing pipes.

**method** - orderly procedure or process; regular manner of doing anything
- Sentence mining is a highly effective method for acquiring natural vocabulary.
- Always document your experimental method so others can reproduce your results.

**methodology** - system of methods followed in a particular discipline
- Reviewers praised the paper's rigorous statistical methodology.
- Agile methodology encourages iterative delivery and frequent feedback.

**meticulous** - excessively careful; marked by extreme care in treatment of details
- Her meticulous attention to formatting made the final report effortless to read.
- Watchmaking demands meticulous precision with microscopic components.

**metric** - relating to measurement; involving, or proceeding by measurement
- Response latency is a key performance metric for interactive web apps.
- Most scientific research uses the metric system for standardized measurements.

**metropolitan** - city center; one who lives in a city center
- High-speed rail connects suburbs quickly across the metropolitan area.
- Metropolitan museums often house vast international art collections.

**microscope** - magnifier of the image of small objects
- Biology students examined plant cells under a compound microscope.
- Putting the proposal under a microscope revealed two subtle edge cases.

### PAGE 104

**microwave** - a short electromagnetic wave; cook or heat in a microwave oven
- Reheat the leftover soup in the microwave for two minutes, stirring halfway.
- Telecommunications towers use microwave signals to transmit data over line-of-sight links.

**mighty** - having or showing great strength or force or intensity
- A mighty oak stood at the center of the village for over three hundred years.
- Even a mighty river begins as a small mountain stream.

**migrant** - habitually moving from place to place especially in search of seasonal work; wandering
- Migrant songbirds travel thousands of miles each autumn toward warmer climates.
- Seasonal migrant workers help harvest crops across agricultural valleys.

**migrate** - move from one country or region to another and settle there
- Monarch butterflies migrate south every fall in massive colorful flocks.
- We plan to migrate the legacy database to a faster storage engine next month.

**migration** - movement of persons from one country or locality to another
- Bird migration routes follow coastlines, rivers, and mountain ridges.
- Careful schema validation ensures a smooth data migration with zero downtime.

**milestone** - landmark; significant development
- Passing all integration tests marked a major milestone in the project timeline.
- Graduating from university is an unforgettable personal milestone.

**military** - pertaining to soldiers, to arms, or to war; of whole body of soldiers
- Military engineers built a temporary pontoon bridge after floods washed out the road.
- Precision and discipline are hallmarks of military aviation.

**milk** - white fluid secreted by the mammary glands of female mammals for the nourishment of their young
- Pour cold milk over your morning oatmeal or stir a splash into hot coffee.
- Fresh milk stays good longer when stored on an interior refrigerator shelf rather than the door.

**miller** - one who keeps or attends a flour mill or gristmill; milling machine
- The village miller ground harvested wheat into fine whole-grain flour.
- Machinists used a precision miller to shape the aluminum housing.

**millionaire** - one whose material wealth is valued at more than a million dollars
- Living frugally and investing steadily helped the retired teacher become a millionaire.
- The self-made millionaire funded scholarships for local engineering students.

**mince** - cut into very small pieces; chop fine; suppress or weaken the force of
- Mince two cloves of garlic finely before sautéing them in olive oil.
- She never minces words when giving honest feedback on a draft.

**mingle** - be all mixed up or jumbled together
- Hosts encouraged guests to mingle over refreshments before the keynote started.
- Warm aromas of cinnamon and roasted coffee mingled in the morning air.

**miniature** - very small; model that represents something in a greatly reduced size
- Hobbyists painted intricate details onto miniature railway models.
- Modern smartphones pack miniature sensors that rival dedicated lab instruments.

**minimize** - diminish; belittle; make small or insignificant
- Caching frequent lookups helps minimize redundant network requests.
- Good ergonomics minimize wrist and neck strain during long desk sessions.

**minimum** - the smallest possible quantity
- Keep external dependencies to a minimum so the script stays portable.
- Applicants need a minimum of two years of practical experience for the role.

**minister** - one who performs service for another; officer of justice; executive head of a government department
- The minister of energy announced new incentives for solar microgrids.
- Community volunteers minister to elderly neighbors during winter storms.

**ministry** - act of serving; government department, at the administrative level normally headed by a minister
- The ministry of education released updated guidelines for digital classrooms.
- She spent a decade working in public policy within the health ministry.

**minor** - of lesser importance or stature or rank; not of legal age; limited in size or scope
- We fixed a few minor typos in the documentation before publishing.
- A minor delay at the station did not affect our connecting train.

**minority** - a group of people who differ racially or politically from a larger group
- Only a small minority of respondents preferred the older interface design.
- Protecting minority viewpoints leads to healthier, more thorough team debates.

**minus** - less; requiring to be subtracted; negative
- Ten minus four equals six.
- Winter temperatures often drop to minus five degrees Celsius overnight.

**minute** - extremely small; short note
- Even minute dust particles can interfere with delicate optical lenses.
- Give me just one minute to save these files before we head out.

**miracle** - wonderful thing; something that excites admiration or astonishment
- Finding an uncorrupted backup after a sudden disk failure felt like a miracle.
- Modern vaccines are a scientific miracle that has saved millions of lives.

**miraculous** - astonishing; amazing
- The hiker made a miraculous recovery after spending two nights in freezing weather.
- Seedlings staged a miraculous comeback once spring rain finally arrived.

**miscarriage** - failure of administration or management; failure to attain a desired result or reach a destination
- Suppressing key evidence resulted in a serious miscarriage of justice.
- Poor coordination led to a complete miscarriage of the logistics plan.

**mischief** - behavior that causes discomfiture or annoyance in another; tendency to play pranks or cause embarrassment
- The playful kitten was full of mischief, batting pens off the desk all afternoon.
- Keep curious toddlers out of mischief with engaging wooden puzzles.
